Sri Lanka Export Development Board/ Export Performance Indicators 2025 
 
 
 
FORWARD 
 
Exports are a key driver of economic growth, foreign exchange generation, employment, productivity, 
and competitiveness. In the context of Sri Lanka’s economic transformation, moving towards a private 
sector-led, export- and FDI-driven development strategy is essential for achieving sustainable growth, 
strengthening external resilience, and deepening integration with the global economy. 
 
In this context, the EDB is pleased to present the 39th Volume of its flagship statistical publication, 
“Export Performance Indicators 2025.” The publication provides a comprehensive overview of Sri 
Lanka’s external trade and export performance, including the contribution of exports to key economic 
indicators such as GDP and the trade balance. It also provides insights into the performance of major 
export sectors, products, and markets. 
 
The publication presents export data for 2025 and the preceding five years, while selected key 
indicators are provided for a ten-year historical period, enabling users to identify emerging trends and 
assess long-term developments in Sri Lanka’s export sector. Export values are presented in United 
States Dollars (USD) and analysed by export regions, markets, products, and product groups. The EDB 
classification covers 20 global regions, 100 markets, and 179 products/product groups, supported by 
percentage shares and annual growth rates for ease of analysis and comparison. 
 
Over the years, Export Performance Indicators has become a trusted and widely used reference for 
policymakers, researchers, exporters, investors, development partners, and other stakeholders. The 
publication is intended to support evidence-based policy formulation, strategic decision-making, 
market analysis, and a better understanding of Sri Lanka’s export performance. 
 
The EDB gratefully acknowledges Sri Lanka Customs and other government institutions and agencies 
for their valuable cooperation and for providing the data and information required for the preparation 
of this publication. The EDB also welcomes feedback and suggestions from users to continuously 
improve the accuracy, relevance, and usefulness of this publication. 
 
The Policy & Strategic Planning Division of the EDB coordinated the preparation of this publication. I 
extend my sincere appreciation to all EDB officers, government institutions, agencies, and other 
stakeholders whose valuable contributions, expertise, and cooperation supported the successful 
completion of this publication. 
 
 
Mangala Wijesinghe 
Chairman / Chief Executive Officer 
Sri Lanka Export Development Board 
 
 
i 

--- page 3 ---
Sri Lanka Export Development Board/ Export Performance Indicators 2025 
 
 
 
 
ABOUT EDB 
 
 
The Sri Lanka Export Development Board (EDB) is the apex government institution 
responsible for the development and promotion of Sri Lanka’s exports. Since its 
establishment in 1979, the EDB has played a pivotal role in strengthening the country’s export 
sector. Over more than four decades, its mandate and scope have evolved beyond traditional 
merchandise exports to encompass a broader range of export products and services, while 
responding to changing global trade dynamics, emerging market opportunities, technological 
developments, and evolving international market requirements. 
 
As a key institution driving Sri Lanka’s export agenda, the EDB serves as a Policy Advisor, 
Monitor, Facilitator, Knowledge Provider, and Promoter, working closely with government 
institutions, the private sector, industry associations, development partners, and other 
stakeholders. Its activities encompass export policy and strategy, market intelligence, sector 
development, trade promotion, exporter development, capacity building, research, and 
international market access. 
 
The EDB is governed by a Board of Directors representing key government ministries and 
leading private sector stakeholders, providing strategic direction and strengthening public-
private sector collaboration. Its professional workforce brings expertise in trade 
development, market intelligence, sector development, research, capacity building, and 
international business. 
 
A key priority of the EDB is to promote inclusive, diversified, and regionally balanced export 
development. Through island-wide outreach and regional programmes, the Board supports 
Small and Medium Enterprises (SMEs), emerging exporters, and potential exporters by 
providing advisory services, training, market intelligence, awareness programmes, and 
buyer–seller linkages to strengthen export readiness and expand participation in 
international trade. 
 
The EDB also promotes product and market diversification, value addition, innovation, 
digitalization, sustainability, and integration into global value chains. By monitoring emerging 
trends in international trade, changing consumer preferences, technological developments, 
sustainability requirements, and international market regulations, the EDB supports Sri 
Lankan enterprises in adapting to an increasingly competitive global environment. 
 
Through these efforts, the EDB seeks to build a diversified, resilient, inclusive, and globally 
competitive export sector, while creating greater opportunities for businesses across the 
country to participate in international trade. By connecting local capabilities with global 
opportunities, the EDB contributes to strengthening Sri Lanka’s export base and supporting 
sustainable long-term economic development.

28 
 
 
 
 
Concentration index is a statistical parameter which provides a measurement of the market 
concentration of certain product, a product sector or of all products of a country as a whole. Ideally 
lower the market concentrations lower the risk for exports due to market failure. Market should 
be discrete and the share of each market should be fairly equal, so that the collapse of one or a 
few markets will not seriously affect the product’s overall exports. To analyse the said scenario and 
evaluate the concentration index the following formula has been used. 
 
 
 
Concentration Index  = 
                (HHi) 
 
 
Xi = A Product sector’s exports to country i (i = UK, USA, Italy…) 
X = Total of a product sector’s exports to all countries (X = Xi + .. + Xn) 
n = Number of buyer countries 
 
This formula takes into account the export share in each market of a particular product and 
calculates an index which can vary between the limits zero and hundred. The optimal situation of 
the index would be when the concentration index is zero, which is when the shares are exactly 
equal among all the markets. At the other extreme, the value goes up to 100 if the market shares 
are drastically different from each other with the difference between the highest share and the 
lowest share being considerably large. 
 
The calculated indices for the main export product sectors of Sri Lanka are tabulated in page 29 : 
 
19. MARKET CONCENTRATION OF SRI LANKA’S EXPORT PRODUCT SECTOR

The table reveals that all product sectors have diversified their number of destinations over the period of 1990 – 2025.
Total merchandise expanded from 136 destinations in 1990 to 212 in 2025, while the overall export market concentration
index improved slightly from 23.4% to 22.4% indicating reduce dependence on a few markets.
Although most sectors demonstrate improved or stable market diversification, sectors such as Rubber & Rubber based
products, Food & Beverages and Boat Building, continue to exhibit relatively high market concentration despite or increase
in the number of export destinations.

THE HERFINDHAL HIRSCHMAN INDEX 
This index is widely used to measure the 
concentration of products mix exported by the 
country and is defined as, 
              
 
Where xi is the export of commodity, X the 
country's total exports and n the number of 
export products. Index (%) approaching zero 
indicates less degree of concentration and index 
(%) approaching 100 implies a high degree of 
concentration. 
 
Lower HHI                   Greater export product 
diversification.

27.  CURRENT TREND IN GLOBAL DEMAND FOR SRI LANKA’S PRODUCTS - 2025 
 
 
The following chart shows the performance of Sri Lanka’s top 20 export product categories at 
the 2-digit HS Code level in the global market in 2025. Each bubble represents a specific 
product category, while the size of the bubble indicates the value of Sri Lanka’s exports of 
that product to the world market, in US dollars. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Source: ITC Trade map 
 
If the bubbles fall within the “Winners in Growing Sectors” category, indicating both high 
global import growth and an increasing share of Sri Lanka’s exports, it means that global 
demand for the product is growing while Sri Lanka is also strengthening its position in the 
world market. For example, Preparations of vegetables, fruit, nuts or other parts of plants (HS 
20) and Miscellaneous edible preparations (HS 21) fall into this category. Trade promotion 
efforts for these products should focus on expanding supply capacity, strengthening market 
access, and supporting exporters to meet the growing demand in international markets. 
 
 
 

--- page 356 ---
Sri Lanka Export Development Board/Export Performance Indicators 2025 
342 
 
If the bubbles fall into the “Losers in Growing Sectors” category, it means that global demand 
for the product is increasing, but Sri Lanka’s share of world exports is declining. For example, 
Coffee, tea, maté and spices (HS 09); Natural or cultured pearls, precious or semi-precious 
stones, precious metals and metals clad (HS 71); Other vegetable textile fibres, paper yarn 
and woven fabrics of paper yarn (HS 53); Edible fruit and nuts; peel of citrus fruit or melons 
(HS 08); and Tobacco and manufactured tobacco substitutes (HS 24) fall into this category. As 
these products may face supply-side constraints and other competitiveness challenges, it is 
important to identify and address the underlying bottlenecks in order to regain and 
strengthen Sri Lanka’s share of the growing global market. 
 
If the bubbles fall into the “Losers in Declining Sectors” category, it means that global demand 
for the product is declining and Sri Lanka’s share of world exports is also decreasing. For 
example, Articles of apparel and clothing accessories, knitted or crocheted (HS 61); Articles of 
apparel and clothing accessories, not knitted or crocheted (HS 62); Rubber and articles 
thereof (HS 40); and Fish and crustaceans, molluscs and other aquatic invertebrates (HS 03) 
fall into this category. Trade promotion efforts for these products should adopt an 
integrated approach that addresses both demand and supply-side constraints, while 
focusing on improving competitiveness, product diversification, and exploring new market 
opportunities. 
 
If the bubbles fall into the “Winners in Declining Sectors” category, it means that global 
demand for the product is declining, but Sri Lanka’s share of world exports is increasing, 
indicating that Sri Lanka is strengthening its competitive position despite a shrinking global 
market. For example, Other made-up textile articles; sets; worn clothing and worn textile 
articles; rags (HS 63) fall into this category. From a trade promotion perspective, targeted 
niche marketing strategies should be adopted to maintain Sri Lanka’s competitive position, 
retain existing markets, and identify specialized market segments with potential for 
sustainable growth. 
 
The export performance patterns highlighted above demonstrate the importance of adopting 
product-specific trade promotion strategies and implementing targeted measures to address 
the specific demand and supply-side constraints that hinder the dynamic expansion of Sri 
Lanka’s exports. Such strategies would enable Sri Lanka to better capitalize on opportunities 
in the global market, strengthen its export competitiveness, and maximize the benefits arising 
from international trade opportunities for its export products.
